[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the biggest reason I came in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like, not just tired or distracted—I mean I can sit at my desk staring at one audit workpaper for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to type anything. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it keeps happening.\n\nI’m an accountant, so I’m used to being careful and methodical, but this feels different. It’s like everything takes this enormous amount of effort, even starting a small task. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, and that’s… embarrassing, honestly. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s getting hard to function normally.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this slowing down?\n\nPatient: About seven months, I think. It kind of started around when my husband stopped working after the downsizing at his company. At first I thought I was just stressed and trying to adjust, because everything at home felt like it shifted all at once, but looking back, that’s when I started noticing I was moving and thinking more slowly. It’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed. It’s not just “I’m having a bad morning” or being stressed about work. It feels like I open my eyes and I already know I’m going to have to drag myself through the whole day.\n\nAnd it’s hard to explain because sometimes nothing has happened yet—no email, no conversation, no actual problem in that moment—but I still feel this weight there. It’s just there immediately, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. I keep thinking maybe if I get moving it will lift, but a lot of the time it just stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s changed a lot. I used to have things I actually looked forward to, and now I don’t really feel that pull anymore.\n\nLike, I used to plan hikes ahead of time—look up trails, check the weather, pack snacks, make a whole little plan out of it. I used to take photos when I went, not professionally or anything, just because I liked noticing things. And Sundays, I’d usually cook something more involved, like I’d make a big pot of soup or try a new recipe. I also volunteered at the library, and that used to feel meaningful to me.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… items on a checklist. Like things I know I used to care about, but I can’t seem to care enough to actually start them. Even if I have time, I just look at the idea of doing it and it feels flat, like there’s no reward in it. I keep thinking I should make myself do those things because maybe I’ll feel better, but then it just becomes another thing I’m failing to get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. I mean… I do worry, but it’s hard for me to separate that from everything else. It’s not like I’m having sudden panic or feeling keyed up all the time, at least not in a way I can clearly point to.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like my mind gets stuck on things—whether I’m messing things up, whether I’m behind, whether I’m not doing enough—and then I sort of turn it over and over. But I’m not sure if that’s “anxious” the way you mean it. It feels heavier than nervous, if that makes sense. More like dread, maybe, or just this constant sense that I can’t handle things the way I used to.\n\nSo… yes, I worry some, but I don’t know that worry is the main thing I notice. It’s more just there in the background with everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve sat there and made some kind of clear plan, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments where I think… I don’t know… like it would be easier if I just didn’t have to wake up and do another day. It’s not always dramatic, and it’s not like I’m constantly saying “I want to die” in my head, but sometimes there’s this feeling of being so tired of myself and tired of trying that the idea of not being here feels like… relief, almost.\n\nThat’s hard to say out loud because it sounds worse when I say it. Most of the time I just keep it in my head and then feel guilty for even thinking that way. I don’t know if that counts as wishing I were dead, exactly. I guess maybe sometimes it’s close to that. It’s more like I don’t want to keep feeling like this, and I don’t know how to separate that from not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it, though I didn’t really track it at first.\n\nFood has kind of become this thing I do because I know I’m supposed to, not because I actually want it. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. Sometimes I end up forcing down a few bites because I know skipping completely probably isn’t good, but it doesn’t feel satisfying.\n\nAnd my clothes are definitely different. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. If anything, it sort of surprised me when I noticed, because I wasn’t thinking of it as a “weight loss” thing at first. I just realized I kept adjusting them or they weren’t sitting the way they used to. So… yes, I think I’ve lost some weight, but not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, that’s one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong.\n\nIt’s not just that I feel tired or unmotivated. It’s like my whole body and brain are moving through mud. At work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it, and I know what I’m supposed to do—I mean, I used to be able to move through that kind of thing pretty automatically—but now I can sit there for twenty minutes before I even get my hand to move to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. Even speaking sometimes feels slower, like I have to drag the words out or it takes me longer to answer than it should.\n\nSo… yes, definitely. I feel slowed down, and it’s affecting my work enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, guilty maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI do spend a lot of time feeling like I’m failing at things that should be normal. Like I’ll tell myself I’m being lazy or difficult, even though another part of me knows that’s probably not fair. And then I feel guilty for not being more like myself, or for making things harder for people around me, or for not handling things better. It’s this kind of constant background feeling that I should be doing more, fixing it faster, not needing anyone to be patient with me.\n\nWorthless is a harder word. I don’t usually sit there and say, “I’m worthless,” exactly. But I do have moments where I feel like I’m not bringing much to anything, or like I’m just… disappointing. It’s uncomfortable to admit because it sounds so self-pitying, and I hate that. Then I feel guilty for even talking about it, like I’m making too much of myself.\n\nSo yes, I guess there is guilt. I’m less sure about worthless, but there are times I feel pretty low about who I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. But the question makes me uncomfortable because I do sometimes think about death in a kind of… abstract way, I guess? Like it crosses my mind more than it used to, but I don’t always know whether that counts as “thoughts about death or dying” or if it’s just me feeling really worn down and thinking about how exhausting everything feels.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve really wanted to say out loud, because it sounds alarming, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I guess, yes, there are times when my mind goes there. Not necessarily in a clear or organized way. More like this heavy thought of… I don’t know, what if I didn’t have to keep doing this, or wondering about death in this detached way. Then I feel bad for even thinking it and try to push it away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about it every minute, but it has come up repeatedly enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about acting on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. The thoughts are more like… I’ll be lying there or sitting somewhere and think, “I don’t want to keep feeling like this,” or “I wish I didn’t have to wake up and do another day,” but it doesn’t turn into a step-by-step thing in my head. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. Some of my work skirts have gotten loose around the waist and hips, and they weren’t like that before. I keep having to adjust them or they sit lower than they used to. And I’m not trying to lose weight — I’m not dieting or exercising more or anything like that.\n\nFood has just become… kind of an obligation. Like I know I’m supposed to eat, so I’ll force something down because it’s “time” or because I realize I haven’t had anything, but I don’t really want it. It’s not enjoyable. A lot of the time I’ll look at food and it feels like one more task I have to complete, which sounds ridiculous, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, yes, I think I’ve lost weight over the last several months. I can’t say for sure if it’s more than 5%, but it’s enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes, and it wasn’t intentional.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. The thoughts are more like… I’ll be lying there or sitting somewhere and think, “I don’t want to keep feeling like this,” or “I wish I didn’t have to wake up and do another day,” but it doesn’t turn into a step-by-step thing in my head. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. Some of my work skirts have gotten loose around the waist and hips, and they weren’t like that before. I keep having to adjust them or they sit lower than they used to. And I’m not trying to lose weight — I’m not dieting or exercising more or anything like that.\n\nFood has just become… kind of an obligation. Like I know I’m supposed to eat, so I’ll force something down because it’s “time” or because I realize I haven’t had anything, but I don’t really want it. It’s not enjoyable. A lot of the time I’ll look at food and it feels like one more task I have to complete, which sounds ridiculous, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, yes, I think I’ve lost weight over the last several months. Not really restless, no. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.

I don’t feel like I need to pace or move around more than usual. What’s been scaring me is how slowed down I feel. Like at work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the biggest reason I came in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like, not just tired or distracted—I mean I can sit at my desk staring at one audit workpaper for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to type anything. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it keeps happening.\n\nI’m an accountant, so I’m used to being careful and methodical, but this feels different. It’s like everything takes this enormous amount of effort, even starting a small task. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, and that’s… embarrassing, honestly. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s getting hard to function normally.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this slowing down?\n\nPatient: About seven months, I think. It kind of started around when my husband stopped working after the downsizing at his company. At first I thought I was just stressed and trying to adjust, because everything at home felt like it shifted all at once, but looking back, that’s when I started noticing I was moving and thinking more slowly. It’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed. It’s not just “I’m having a bad morning” or being stressed about work. It feels like I open my eyes and I already know I’m going to have to drag myself through the whole day.\n\nAnd it’s hard to explain because sometimes nothing has happened yet—no email, no conversation, no actual problem in that moment—but I still feel this weight there. It’s just there immediately, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. I keep thinking maybe if I get moving it will lift, but a lot of the time it just stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s changed a lot. I used to have things I actually looked forward to, and now I don’t really feel that pull anymore.\n\nLike, I used to plan hikes ahead of time—look up trails, check the weather, pack snacks, make a whole little plan out of it. I used to take photos when I went, not professionally or anything, just because I liked noticing things. And Sundays, I’d usually cook something more involved, like I’d make a big pot of soup or try a new recipe. I also volunteered at the library, and that used to feel meaningful to me.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… items on a checklist. Like things I know I used to care about, but I can’t seem to care enough to actually start them. Even if I have time, I just look at the idea of doing it and it feels flat, like there’s no reward in it. I keep thinking I should make myself do those things because maybe I’ll feel better, but then it just becomes another thing I’m failing to get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. I mean… I do worry, but it’s hard for me to separate that from everything else. It’s not like I’m having sudden panic or feeling keyed up all the time, at least not in a way I can clearly point to.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like my mind gets stuck on things—whether I’m messing things up, whether I’m behind, whether I’m not doing enough—and then I sort of turn it over and over. But I’m not sure if that’s “anxious” the way you mean it. It feels heavier than nervous, if that makes sense. More like dread, maybe, or just this constant sense that I can’t handle things the way I used to.\n\nSo… yes, I worry some, but I don’t know that worry is the main thing I notice. It’s more just there in the background with everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve sat there and made some kind of clear plan, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments where I think… I don’t know… like it would be easier if I just didn’t have to wake up and do another day. It’s not always dramatic, and it’s not like I’m constantly saying “I want to die” in my head, but sometimes there’s this feeling of being so tired of myself and tired of trying that the idea of not being here feels like… relief, almost.\n\nThat’s hard to say out loud because it sounds worse when I say it. Most of the time I just keep it in my head and then feel guilty for even thinking that way. I don’t know if that counts as wishing I were dead, exactly. I guess maybe sometimes it’s close to that. It’s more like I don’t want to keep feeling like this, and I don’t know how to separate that from not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it, though I didn’t really track it at first.\n\nFood has kind of become this thing I do because I know I’m supposed to, not because I actually want it. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. Sometimes I end up forcing down a few bites because I know skipping completely probably isn’t good, but it doesn’t feel satisfying.\n\nAnd my clothes are definitely different. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. If anything, it sort of surprised me when I noticed, because I wasn’t thinking of it as a “weight loss” thing at first. I just realized I kept adjusting them or they weren’t sitting the way they used to. So… yes, I think I’ve lost some weight, but not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, that’s one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong.\n\nIt’s not just that I feel tired or unmotivated. It’s like my whole body and brain are moving through mud. At work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it, and I know what I’m supposed to do—I mean, I used to be able to move through that kind of thing pretty automatically—but now I can sit there for twenty minutes before I even get my hand to move to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. Even speaking sometimes feels slower, like I have to drag the words out or it takes me longer to answer than it should.\n\nSo… yes, definitely. I feel slowed down, and it’s affecting my work enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, guilty maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI do spend a lot of time feeling like I’m failing at things that should be normal. Like I’ll tell myself I’m being lazy or difficult, even though another part of me knows that’s probably not fair. And then I feel guilty for not being more like myself, or for making things harder for people around me, or for not handling things better. It’s this kind of constant background feeling that I should be doing more, fixing it faster, not needing anyone to be patient with me.\n\nWorthless is a harder word. I don’t usually sit there and say, “I’m worthless,” exactly. But I do have moments where I feel like I’m not bringing much to anything, or like I’m just… disappointing. It’s uncomfortable to admit because it sounds so self-pitying, and I hate that. Then I feel guilty for even talking about it, like I’m making too much of myself.\n\nSo yes, I guess there is guilt. I’m less sure about worthless, but there are times I feel pretty low about who I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. But the question makes me uncomfortable because I do sometimes think about death in a kind of… abstract way, I guess? Like it crosses my mind more than it used to, but I don’t always know whether that counts as “thoughts about death or dying” or if it’s just me feeling really worn down and thinking about how exhausting everything feels.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve really wanted to say out loud, because it sounds alarming, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I guess, yes, there are times when my mind goes there. Not necessarily in a clear or organized way. More like this heavy thought of… I don’t know, what if I didn’t have to keep doing this, or wondering about death in this detached way. Then I feel bad for even thinking it and try to push it away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about it every minute, but it has come up repeatedly enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about acting on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. The thoughts are more like… I’ll be lying there or sitting somewhere and think, “I don’t want to keep feeling like this,” or “I wish I didn’t have to wake up and do another day,” but it doesn’t turn into a step-by-step thing in my head. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. Some of my work skirts have gotten loose around the waist and hips, and they weren’t like that before. I keep having to adjust them or they sit lower than they used to. And I’m not trying to lose weight — I’m not dieting or exercising more or anything like that.\n\nFood has just become… kind of an obligation. Like I know I’m supposed to eat, so I’ll force something down because it’s “time” or because I realize I haven’t had anything, but I don’t really want it. It’s not enjoyable. A lot of the time I’ll look at food and it feels like one more task I have to complete, which sounds ridiculous, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, yes, I think I’ve lost weight over the last several months. I can’t say for sure if it’s more than 5%, but it’s enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes, and it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\nPatient: Not really restless, no. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.\n\nI don’t feel like I need to pace or move around more than usual. What’s been scaring me is how slowed down I feel. Like at work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it. I know what I need to do, I know where my hand needs to go, but it’s like there’s this huge delay before I can even start moving. I’ve sat there for twenty minutes before I even type one thing, which sounds absurd when I say it out loud, but that’s actually happening.\n\nAnd people are noticing I’m not keeping up. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not like tapping or needing to move my legs or anything like that. I don’t really get that keyed-up feeling where I have to keep moving.

It’s more the opposite, and that’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here. I feel so slowed down that I’ll be at my desk looking at an audit workpaper, and I know I need to type something or click into a cell or start reviewing it, but I just… don’t move. I can sit there for twenty minutes staring at it before my hand even goes to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds like I’m exaggerating or just procrastinating, but it feels different from that.

And people at work have started noticing that I’m not keeping up. It’s not that I’m fidgeting too much or can’t sit still — it’s like I’m stuck in place.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the biggest reason I came in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like, not just tired or distracted—I mean I can sit at my desk staring at one audit workpaper for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to type anything. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it keeps happening.\n\nI’m an accountant, so I’m used to being careful and methodical, but this feels different. It’s like everything takes this enormous amount of effort, even starting a small task. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, and that’s… embarrassing, honestly. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s getting hard to function normally.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this slowing down?\n\nPatient: About seven months, I think. It kind of started around when my husband stopped working after the downsizing at his company. At first I thought I was just stressed and trying to adjust, because everything at home felt like it shifted all at once, but looking back, that’s when I started noticing I was moving and thinking more slowly. It’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed. It’s not just “I’m having a bad morning” or being stressed about work. It feels like I open my eyes and I already know I’m going to have to drag myself through the whole day.\n\nAnd it’s hard to explain because sometimes nothing has happened yet—no email, no conversation, no actual problem in that moment—but I still feel this weight there. It’s just there immediately, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. I keep thinking maybe if I get moving it will lift, but a lot of the time it just stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s changed a lot. I used to have things I actually looked forward to, and now I don’t really feel that pull anymore.\n\nLike, I used to plan hikes ahead of time—look up trails, check the weather, pack snacks, make a whole little plan out of it. I used to take photos when I went, not professionally or anything, just because I liked noticing things. And Sundays, I’d usually cook something more involved, like I’d make a big pot of soup or try a new recipe. I also volunteered at the library, and that used to feel meaningful to me.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… items on a checklist. Like things I know I used to care about, but I can’t seem to care enough to actually start them. Even if I have time, I just look at the idea of doing it and it feels flat, like there’s no reward in it. I keep thinking I should make myself do those things because maybe I’ll feel better, but then it just becomes another thing I’m failing to get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. I mean… I do worry, but it’s hard for me to separate that from everything else. It’s not like I’m having sudden panic or feeling keyed up all the time, at least not in a way I can clearly point to.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like my mind gets stuck on things—whether I’m messing things up, whether I’m behind, whether I’m not doing enough—and then I sort of turn it over and over. But I’m not sure if that’s “anxious” the way you mean it. It feels heavier than nervous, if that makes sense. More like dread, maybe, or just this constant sense that I can’t handle things the way I used to.\n\nSo… yes, I worry some, but I don’t know that worry is the main thing I notice. It’s more just there in the background with everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve sat there and made some kind of clear plan, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments where I think… I don’t know… like it would be easier if I just didn’t have to wake up and do another day. It’s not always dramatic, and it’s not like I’m constantly saying “I want to die” in my head, but sometimes there’s this feeling of being so tired of myself and tired of trying that the idea of not being here feels like… relief, almost.\n\nThat’s hard to say out loud because it sounds worse when I say it. Most of the time I just keep it in my head and then feel guilty for even thinking that way. I don’t know if that counts as wishing I were dead, exactly. I guess maybe sometimes it’s close to that. It’s more like I don’t want to keep feeling like this, and I don’t know how to separate that from not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it, though I didn’t really track it at first.\n\nFood has kind of become this thing I do because I know I’m supposed to, not because I actually want it. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. Sometimes I end up forcing down a few bites because I know skipping completely probably isn’t good, but it doesn’t feel satisfying.\n\nAnd my clothes are definitely different. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. If anything, it sort of surprised me when I noticed, because I wasn’t thinking of it as a “weight loss” thing at first. I just realized I kept adjusting them or they weren’t sitting the way they used to. So… yes, I think I’ve lost some weight, but not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, that’s one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong.\n\nIt’s not just that I feel tired or unmotivated. It’s like my whole body and brain are moving through mud. At work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it, and I know what I’m supposed to do—I mean, I used to be able to move through that kind of thing pretty automatically—but now I can sit there for twenty minutes before I even get my hand to move to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. Even speaking sometimes feels slower, like I have to drag the words out or it takes me longer to answer than it should.\n\nSo… yes, definitely. I feel slowed down, and it’s affecting my work enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, guilty maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI do spend a lot of time feeling like I’m failing at things that should be normal. Like I’ll tell myself I’m being lazy or difficult, even though another part of me knows that’s probably not fair. And then I feel guilty for not being more like myself, or for making things harder for people around me, or for not handling things better. It’s this kind of constant background feeling that I should be doing more, fixing it faster, not needing anyone to be patient with me.\n\nWorthless is a harder word. I don’t usually sit there and say, “I’m worthless,” exactly. But I do have moments where I feel like I’m not bringing much to anything, or like I’m just… disappointing. It’s uncomfortable to admit because it sounds so self-pitying, and I hate that. Then I feel guilty for even talking about it, like I’m making too much of myself.\n\nSo yes, I guess there is guilt. I’m less sure about worthless, but there are times I feel pretty low about who I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. But the question makes me uncomfortable because I do sometimes think about death in a kind of… abstract way, I guess? Like it crosses my mind more than it used to, but I don’t always know whether that counts as “thoughts about death or dying” or if it’s just me feeling really worn down and thinking about how exhausting everything feels.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve really wanted to say out loud, because it sounds alarming, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I guess, yes, there are times when my mind goes there. Not necessarily in a clear or organized way. More like this heavy thought of… I don’t know, what if I didn’t have to keep doing this, or wondering about death in this detached way. Then I feel bad for even thinking it and try to push it away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about it every minute, but it has come up repeatedly enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about acting on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. It’s more like I don’t want to keep feeling like this, and I don’t know how to separate that from not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it, though I didn’t really track it at first.\n\nFood has kind of become this thing I do because I know I’m supposed to, not because I actually want it. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. Sometimes I end up forcing down a few bites because I know skipping completely probably isn’t good, but it doesn’t feel satisfying.\n\nAnd my clothes are definitely different. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. If anything, it sort of surprised me when I noticed, because I wasn’t thinking of it as a “weight loss” thing at first. I just realized I kept adjusting them or they weren’t sitting the way they used to. So… yes, I think I’ve lost some weight, but not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, that’s one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong.\n\nIt’s not just that I feel tired or unmotivated. It’s like my whole body and brain are moving through mud. At work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it, and I know what I’m supposed to do—I mean, I used to be able to move through that kind of thing pretty automatically—but now I can sit there for twenty minutes before I even get my hand to move to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. Even speaking sometimes feels slower, like I have to drag the words out or it takes me longer to answer than it should.\n\nSo… yes, definitely. I feel slowed down, and it’s affecting my work enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, guilty maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI do spend a lot of time feeling like I’m failing at things that should be normal. Like I’ll tell myself I’m being lazy or difficult, even though another part of me knows that’s probably not fair. And then I feel guilty for not being more like myself, or for making things harder for people around me, or for not handling things better. It’s this kind of constant background feeling that I should be doing more, fixing it faster, not needing anyone to be patient with me.\n\nWorthless is a harder word. I don’t usually sit there and say, “I’m worthless,” exactly. But I do have moments where I feel like I’m not bringing much to anything, or like I’m just… disappointing. It’s uncomfortable to admit because it sounds so self-pitying, and I hate that. Then I feel guilty for even talking about it, like I’m making too much of myself.\n\nSo yes, I guess there is guilt. I’m less sure about worthless, but there are times I feel pretty low about who I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. But the question makes me uncomfortable because I do sometimes think about death in a kind of… abstract way, I guess? Like it crosses my mind more than it used to, but I don’t always know whether that counts as “thoughts about death or dying” or if it’s just me feeling really worn down and thinking about how exhausting everything feels.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve really wanted to say out loud, because it sounds alarming, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I guess, yes, there are times when my mind goes there. Not necessarily in a clear or organized way. More like this heavy thought of… I don’t know, what if I didn’t have to keep doing this, or wondering about death in this detached way. Then I feel bad for even thinking it and try to push it away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about it every minute, but it has come up repeatedly enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about acting on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. The thoughts are more like… I’ll be lying there or sitting somewhere and think, “I don’t want to keep feeling like this,” or “I wish I didn’t have to wake up and do another day,” but it doesn’t turn into a step-by-step thing in my head. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. Some of my work skirts have gotten loose around the waist and hips, and they weren’t like that before. I keep having to adjust them or they sit lower than they used to. And I’m not trying to lose weight — I’m not dieting or exercising more or anything like that.\n\nFood has just become… kind of an obligation. Like I know I’m supposed to eat, so I’ll force something down because it’s “time” or because I realize I haven’t had anything, but I don’t really want it. It’s not enjoyable. A lot of the time I’ll look at food and it feels like one more task I have to complete, which sounds ridiculous, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, yes, I think I’ve lost weight over the last several months. I can’t say for sure if it’s more than 5%, but it’s enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes, and it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\nPatient: Not really restless, no. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.\n\nI don’t feel like I need to pace or move around more than usual. What’s been scaring me is how slowed down I feel. Like at work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it. I know what I need to do, I know where my hand needs to go, but it’s like there’s this huge delay before I can even start moving. I’ve sat there for twenty minutes before I even type one thing, which sounds absurd when I say it out loud, but that’s actually happening.\n\nAnd people are noticing I’m not keeping up. I’m not bouncing around or unable to sit still — I’m stuck. Everything takes me so long to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an intense urge to move parts of your body, like tapping your feet or legs?\n\nPatient: No, not like tapping or needing to move my legs or anything like that. No, I don’t feel driven to run or exercise or do physical activity like that. If anything, it’s the opposite of that.

What brings me in is that I’ve gotten so slowed down that even basic work tasks feel like they have this huge pause built into them. I’ll be sitting at my desk with an audit workpaper open, and I know I need to start reviewing it or type something into the file, but I just… sit there. I can stare at it for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to the keyboard.

I feel stuck, like my body isn’t responding the way it’s supposed to. And people at work have started noticing that I’m not keeping up, which is part of why I finally came in.

Even if I have time, I just look at the idea of doing it and it feels flat, like there’s no reward in it. I keep thinking I should make myself do those things because maybe I’ll feel better, but then it just becomes another thing I’m failing to get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. I mean… I do worry, but it’s hard for me to separate that from everything else. It’s not like I’m having sudden panic or feeling keyed up all the time, at least not in a way I can clearly point to.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like my mind gets stuck on things—whether I’m messing things up, whether I’m behind, whether I’m not doing enough—and then I sort of turn it over and over. But I’m not sure if that’s “anxious” the way you mean it. It feels heavier than nervous, if that makes sense. More like dread, maybe, or just this constant sense that I can’t handle things the way I used to.\n\nSo… yes, I worry some, but I don’t know that worry is the main thing I notice. It’s more just there in the background with everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve sat there and made some kind of clear plan, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments where I think… I don’t know… like it would be easier if I just didn’t have to wake up and do another day. It’s not always dramatic, and it’s not like I’m constantly saying “I want to die” in my head, but sometimes there’s this feeling of being so tired of myself and tired of trying that the idea of not being here feels like… relief, almost.\n\nThat’s hard to say out loud because it sounds worse when I say it. Most of the time I just keep it in my head and then feel guilty for even thinking that way. I don’t know if that counts as wishing I were dead, exactly. I guess maybe sometimes it’s close to that. It’s more like I don’t want to keep feeling like this, and I don’t know how to separate that from not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it, though I didn’t really track it at first.\n\nFood has kind of become this thing I do because I know I’m supposed to, not because I actually want it. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. Sometimes I end up forcing down a few bites because I know skipping completely probably isn’t good, but it doesn’t feel satisfying.\n\nAnd my clothes are definitely different. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. If anything, it sort of surprised me when I noticed, because I wasn’t thinking of it as a “weight loss” thing at first. I just realized I kept adjusting them or they weren’t sitting the way they used to. So… yes, I think I’ve lost some weight, but not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, that’s one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong.\n\nIt’s not just that I feel tired or unmotivated. It’s like my whole body and brain are moving through mud. At work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it, and I know what I’m supposed to do—I mean, I used to be able to move through that kind of thing pretty automatically—but now I can sit there for twenty minutes before I even get my hand to move to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. Even speaking sometimes feels slower, like I have to drag the words out or it takes me longer to answer than it should.\n\nSo… yes, definitely. I feel slowed down, and it’s affecting my work enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, guilty maybe, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nI do spend a lot of time feeling like I’m failing at things that should be normal. Like I’ll tell myself I’m being lazy or difficult, even though another part of me knows that’s probably not fair. And then I feel guilty for not being more like myself, or for making things harder for people around me, or for not handling things better. It’s this kind of constant background feeling that I should be doing more, fixing it faster, not needing anyone to be patient with me.\n\nWorthless is a harder word. I don’t usually sit there and say, “I’m worthless,” exactly. But I do have moments where I feel like I’m not bringing much to anything, or like I’m just… disappointing. It’s uncomfortable to admit because it sounds so self-pitying, and I hate that. Then I feel guilty for even talking about it, like I’m making too much of myself.\n\nSo yes, I guess there is guilt. I’m less sure about worthless, but there are times I feel pretty low about who I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. But the question makes me uncomfortable because I do sometimes think about death in a kind of… abstract way, I guess? Like it crosses my mind more than it used to, but I don’t always know whether that counts as “thoughts about death or dying” or if it’s just me feeling really worn down and thinking about how exhausting everything feels.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve really wanted to say out loud, because it sounds alarming, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I guess, yes, there are times when my mind goes there. Not necessarily in a clear or organized way. More like this heavy thought of… I don’t know, what if I didn’t have to keep doing this, or wondering about death in this detached way. Then I feel bad for even thinking it and try to push it away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about it every minute, but it has come up repeatedly enough that I noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about acting on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve thought it through in that kind of concrete way.\n\nLike, when I say I sometimes don’t want to wake up, it’s more like this awful wish to just not have to deal with being conscious and feeling like this, if that makes sense. It’s not… I’m not sitting there planning steps or anything. But I also don’t want to make it sound cleaner than it is, because there are moments where the thought scares me a little, like, “Why am I even thinking this way?”\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’ve had the feeling of not wanting to be here, but I’m not sure I’ve really thought about acting on it. Not in a specific way. It’s more passive, more like wanting everything to stop for a while, and then I feel guilty and try to push it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself fidgeting, pacing, or feeling restless, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more the slow-motion side than fidgeting or pacing.\n\nIt’s actually one of the main reasons I finally came in, because it’s started to affect work in a way I can’t really explain away anymore. I’m an accountant, so I’m used to staring at audit workpapers and spreadsheets, but lately I’ll open one and just… sit there. Like I know what I’m supposed to do, I can see the field I need to type into, but it’s like my hand won’t start. I’ve literally sat there for twenty minutes looking at one workpaper before I even move to type anything.\n\nAnd it isn’t that the task is unusually hard. That’s what makes it so frustrating. These are things I normally would just do automatically, or at least slowly but steadily. Now everything feels like it takes this huge amount of effort to begin. Even clicking through files or writing a simple note feels slowed down, like there’s a delay between deciding to do something and actually doing it.\n\nPeople at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, too, which is embarrassing. I can tell they’re trying not to say it too directly, but it’s there. I’m slower, I’m behind, and I can’t just push through it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would act on those feelings of not wanting to wake up or be alive?\n\nPatient: No… not really in the sense of having an idea of how I would do anything.\n\nI know I’ve already sort of said this, but it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m dodging the question. The thoughts are more like… I’ll be lying there or sitting somewhere and think, “I don’t want to keep feeling like this,” or “I wish I didn’t have to wake up and do another day,” but it doesn’t turn into a step-by-step thing in my head. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. Like I don’t want to look directly at it. So I guess my answer is no, I haven’t thought about how I would act on it. It’s more this passive, awful wishing-not-to-exist feeling than an actual plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past seven months, such as more than 5% of your body weight?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I don’t want to pretend I know the exact number.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is how my clothes fit. Some of my work skirts have gotten loose around the waist and hips, and they weren’t like that before. I keep having to adjust them or they sit lower than they used to. And I’m not trying to lose weight — I’m not dieting or exercising more or anything like that.\n\nFood has just become… kind of an obligation. Like I know I’m supposed to eat, so I’ll force something down because it’s “time” or because I realize I haven’t had anything, but I don’t really want it. It’s not enjoyable. A lot of the time I’ll look at food and it feels like one more task I have to complete, which sounds ridiculous, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, yes, I think I’ve lost weight over the last several months. I can’t say for sure if it’s more than 5%, but it’s enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes, and it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having an urge to move around more than usual?\n\nPatient: Not really restless, no. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.\n\nI don’t feel like I need to pace or move around more than usual. What’s been scaring me is how slowed down I feel. Like at work, I’ll open an audit workpaper and just sit there staring at it. I know what I need to do, I know where my hand needs to go, but it’s like there’s this huge delay before I can even start moving. I’ve sat there for twenty minutes before I even type one thing, which sounds absurd when I say it out loud, but that’s actually happening.\n\nAnd people are noticing I’m not keeping up. I’m not bouncing around or unable to sit still — I’m stuck. Everything takes me so long to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an intense urge to move parts of your body, like tapping your feet or legs?\n\nPatient: No, not like tapping or needing to move my legs or anything like that. I don’t really get that keyed-up feeling where I have to keep moving.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, and that’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here. I feel so slowed down that I’ll be at my desk looking at an audit workpaper, and I know I need to type something or click into a cell or start reviewing it, but I just… don’t move. I can sit there for twenty minutes staring at it before my hand even goes to the keyboard. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds like I’m exaggerating or just procrastinating, but it feels different from that.\n\nAnd people at work have started noticing that I’m not keeping up. It’s not that I’m fidgeting too much or can’t sit still — it’s like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an intense need to engage in physical activity, like running or exercising, as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t feel driven to run or exercise or do physical activity like that. If anything, it’s the opposite of that.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I’ve gotten so slowed down that even basic work tasks feel like they have this huge pause built into them. I’ll be sitting at my desk with an audit workpaper open, and I know I need to start reviewing it or type something into the file, but I just… sit there. I can stare at it for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to the keyboard.\n\nIt’s not like I’m going for walks to burn off feelings or needing to move to calm myself down. I’m not having that kind of energy. I feel stuck, like my body isn’t responding the way it’s supposed to. And people at work have started noticing that I’m not keeping up, which is part of why I finally came in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it. I know what I need to do, I know where my hand needs to go, but it’s like there’s this huge delay before I can even start moving. I’m not bouncing around or unable to sit still — I’m stuck. Everything takes me so long to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an intense urge to move parts of your body, like tapping your feet or legs?\n\nPatient: No, not like tapping or needing to move my legs or anything like that. I feel so slowed down that I’ll be at my desk looking at an audit workpaper, and I know I need to type something or click into a cell or start reviewing it, but I just… don’t move. I can sit there for twenty minutes staring at it before my hand even goes to the keyboard. It’s not that I’m fidgeting too much or can’t sit still — it’s like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an intense need to engage in physical activity, like running or exercising, as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t feel driven to run or exercise or do physical activity like that. If anything, it’s the opposite of that.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I’ve gotten so slowed down that even basic work tasks feel like they have this huge pause built into them. I’ll be sitting at my desk with an audit workpaper open, and I know I need to start reviewing it or type something into the file, but I just… sit there. I can stare at it for twenty minutes before I even move my hand to the keyboard.\n\nIt’s not like I’m going for walks to burn off feelings or needing to move to calm myself down. I’m not having that kind of energy. I feel stuck, like my body isn’t responding the way it’s supposed to. And people at work have started noticing that I’m not keeping up, which is part of why I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood over the past seven months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Most days I wake up and it’s already there, before anything has even happened — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It’s like the day is lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed.

I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly how it feels. I’ll open my eyes and there’s this immediate sadness, or emptiness, and I haven’t even had time to think through what I’m upset about yet. It just sort of arrives with me. And then it stays around for most of the day, nearly every day. It’s not really something that comes and goes in a normal way.

It’s not just “I’m having a bad morning” or being stressed about work. It’s just there immediately, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. Like I’ll look at something and know it’s lunchtime, and I’ll think, okay, I should eat something, but it just feels like effort. My work skirts have gotten loose around the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight at all. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside I probably just look like I’m doing nothing.\n\nAnd people have started noticing. Not always in a direct way, but I can tell. I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Someone will ask if I’ve finished something, and I’ll realize I’ve barely started, even though I’ve been sitting with it open in front of me. I don’t find myself thinking, “I would do this” or “I would use that” or anything like that.\n\nAnd honestly, when the thought even starts to get close to that territory, I get scared and sort of pull back from it.
